              - generic: External, opens in a new tab.
              - generic [ref=e100]:
                - text: "PMCID: PMC12856066 PMID:"
                - link "41624249" [ref=e101] [cursor=pointer]:

          - region "Article content" [ref=e102]:
            - generic [ref=e103]:
              - generic [ref=e104]:
                - heading "ABSTRACT" [level=2] [ref=e105]: ABSTRACT
                - paragraph [ref=e106]: Although the physiological level of reactive oxygen species (ROS) is crucial for governing life processes through redox signaling, the excessive accumulation of ROS can contribute to biomolecular damage and pathological state, namely, oxidative stress. This review systematically summarizes the molecular mechanisms underlying the dynamic equilibrium of cellular redox state, including the intracellular sources of ROS and the multilayered antioxidant defense network. When ROS production exceeds the regulatory limits of the antioxidant system, excessive ROS will act on a series of molecular targets and participate in the pathogenesis of diseases. Therapeutic targeting of the redox balance is regarded as an effective strategy for treating oxidative stress‐related diseases, such as supplementation of direct antioxidants and enhancement of endogenous antioxidant defense network. Nevertheless, clinical trials that attempt to delay the onset or progression of such diseases are mostly negative. This review discusses the challenges encountered in the clinical application of antioxidant therapy and highlights the opportunities brought by novel technologies such as intelligent drug delivery system and personalized medicine. By adopting these new technologies, it is expected to overcome the limitations of traditional antioxidant therapy.
                - paragraph [ref=e108]:
                  - strong [ref=e109]: "Keywords:"
                  - text: oxidative damage, oxidative stress, reactive oxygen species, redox signaling, therapeutic targets
              - generic [ref=e110]:
                - separator [ref=e111]
                - paragraph [ref=e112]: Oxidative stress occurs when the reactive oxygen species (ROS) production overwhelms the ROS scavenging, which lead to genomic instability, epigenetic regulation, proteostasis imbalance, and lipid peroxidation, subsequently causing the occurrence and development of numerous diseases.
                - paragraph [ref=e114]:
                  - img "graphic file with name MCO2-7-e70600-g003.jpg" [ref=e115]
              - generic [ref=e116]:
                - heading "1. Introduction" [level=2] [ref=e117]: 1. Introduction
                - paragraph [ref=e118]:
                  - text: Oxidative stress indicates a state of imbalance between oxidation and antioxidation in the organism. The cellular oxidation and reduction system, also known as the redox system, is an indispensable component of various cellular processes. The acquisition of energy is closely related to the redox system, among which energy supply comes from the oxidative respiration within cells [
                  - link "1" [ref=e119] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0001"
                  - text: "]. In this process, a large quantity of reactive oxygen species (ROS) with oxidizing capabilities can be generated. ROS are partially reduced derivatives of molecular oxygen and mainly divided into two groups: highly unstable oxygen free radicals such as superoxide anion radicals (O"
                  - subscript [ref=e120]: "2"
                  - text: •
                  - superscript [ref=e121]: −
                  - text: ) and chemically stable nonradical oxidants such as hydrogen peroxide (H
                  - subscript [ref=e122]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e123]: "2"
                  - text: ) [
                  - link "2" [ref=e124] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0002"
                  - text: "]. ROS can also be scavenged by the antioxidant system, including the antioxidant enzymes and the non‐enzymatic antioxidants ["
                  - link "3" [ref=e125] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0003"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "4" [ref=e126] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0004"
                  - text: "]. Under physiological conditions, there exists a delicate balance between the production and elimination of ROS in the body that keeps ROS at low levels ["
                  - link "5" [ref=e127] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0005"
                  - text: "]. Physiological levels of ROS are essential for various biological processes, such as cell proliferation, differentiation, and apoptosis. However, excessive ROS generation can contribute to molecular damage and pathological state, denoted as oxidative stress ["
                  - link "6" [ref=e128] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0006"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e129]:
                  - text: Oxidative stress has been clarified to be related to the occurrence and progression of numerous diseases, including musculoskeletal diseases, cardiovascular diseases, respiratory diseases, dermatological diseases, neurodegenerative diseases, gastrointestinal diseases, cancer, and the aging process [
                  - link "7" [ref=e130] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0007"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "8" [ref=e131] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0008"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "9" [ref=e132] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0009"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "10" [ref=e133] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0010"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "11" [ref=e134] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0011"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "12" [ref=e135] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0012"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "13" [ref=e136] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0013"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "14" [ref=e137] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0014"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "15" [ref=e138] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0015"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "16" [ref=e139] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0016"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "17" [ref=e140] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0017"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "18" [ref=e141] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0018"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "19" [ref=e142] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0019"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "20" [ref=e143] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0020"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "21" [ref=e144] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0021"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "22" [ref=e145] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0022"
                  - text: "]. The accumulation of ROS can directly damage biomolecules within cells, resulting in cellular dysfunction or even death ["
                  - link "23" [ref=e146] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0023"
                  - text: "]. In addition to direct oxidative damage, the dysregulation of redox system leads to aberrant redox modifications and the activation of downstream signaling pathways, including genomic instability, epigenetic regulation, proteostasis imbalance, and lipid peroxidation ["
                  - link "24" [ref=e147] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0024"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "25" [ref=e148] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0025"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "26" [ref=e149] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0026"
                  - text: "]. Supplementation of direct antioxidants is a classic antioxidant strategy. However, it is extremely difficult to precisely deliver antioxidants to the sites damaged by oxidative stress at the appropriate stage of disease progression ["
                  - link "27" [ref=e150] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0027"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "28" [ref=e151] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0028"
                  - text: "]. Moreover, the reaction rate of most antioxidants with ROS is much slower than that of antioxidant enzymes, and their actual antioxidant efficiency in vivo may be lower than expected ["
                  - link "29" [ref=e152] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0029"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "30" [ref=e153] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0030"
                  - text: "]. Enhancement of endogenous antioxidant defense network, such as the nuclear factor‐E2‐related factor 2 (NRF2) pathway and antioxidant enzymes, seems more promising, but it also needs to confront the problem of off‐target. Given the dual role of oxidative stress in both physiological and pathological processes, overly simplified broad‐spectrum antioxidant interventions may fail to achieve the desired therapeutic effect, which also explains the disappointing results in clinical trials ["
                  - link "31" [ref=e154] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0031"
                  - text: "]. Therefore, the main research goals in the future lie in the intelligent drug delivery system and personalized medicine."
                - paragraph [ref=e155]:
                  - text: This review will focus on elaborating the molecular mechanisms underlying the dynamic equilibrium of cellular redox state, including the intracellular sources of ROS and the multilayered antioxidant defense network, and clarify the molecular targets of oxidative stress and their roles in the pathogenesis of disease. Furthermore, this review points out the limitations of traditional antioxidant treatments, such as supplementation of direct antioxidants and enhancement of endogenous antioxidant defense network, and highlights the opportunities brought by new technologies, aiming to provide directions for future research in this field (Figure
                  - link "1" [ref=e156] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-fig-0001"
                  - text: ).
                - figure "Imbalance of cellular redox homeostasis. Oxidative stress occurs when the reactive oxygen species (ROS) production overwhelms the ROS scavenging, which lead to genomic instability, epigenetic regulation, proteostasis imbalance, and lipid peroxidation. On this basis, oxidative stress is involved in numerous diseases, including musculoskeletal diseases, cardiovascular diseases, respiratory diseases, dermatological diseases, neurodegenerative diseases, gastrointestinal diseases, cancer, and the aging process." [ref=e157]:
                  - heading "FIGURE 1." [level=3] [ref=e158]
                  - paragraph [ref=e159]:
                    - img "FIGURE 1" [ref=e160]
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e162] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/mco270600-fig-0001/
                  - paragraph [ref=e164]: Imbalance of cellular redox homeostasis. Oxidative stress occurs when the reactive oxygen species (ROS) production overwhelms the ROS scavenging, which lead to genomic instability, epigenetic regulation, proteostasis imbalance, and lipid peroxidation. On this basis, oxidative stress is involved in numerous diseases, including musculoskeletal diseases, cardiovascular diseases, respiratory diseases, dermatological diseases, neurodegenerative diseases, gastrointestinal diseases, cancer, and the aging process.
              - generic [ref=e165]:
                - heading "2. The Dynamic Equilibrium of Cellular Redox State" [level=2] [ref=e166]: 2. The Dynamic Equilibrium of Cellular Redox State
                - paragraph [ref=e167]:
                  - text: There are multiple sites within cells that can generate and release ROS, such as mitochondria, endoplasmic reticulum, peroxisome, and plasma membrane [
                  - link "32" [ref=e168] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0032"
                  - text: "]. Meanwhile, the multilayered antioxidant defense network composed of non‐enzymatic antioxidants and antioxidant enzymes within cells prevents the excessive accumulation of ROS ["
                  - link "33" [ref=e169] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0033"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "34" [ref=e170] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0034"
                  - text: "]. The precise regulation of ROS generation and elimination maintain the cellular redox state in a dynamic equilibrium (Figure"
                  - link "2" [ref=e171] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#mco270600-fig-0002"
                  - text: ).
                - figure "The production and elimination of reactive oxygen species (ROS). There are multiple intracellular sources of ROS, including the electron transport chain, monoamine oxidase, P66shc, the process of protein folding, cytochrome P450 enzymes, peroxisomal oxidases, and nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate oxidases (NOXs). Correspondingly, there are various non‐enzymatic antioxidants and antioxidant enzymes within the cells, including superoxide dismutase (SOD), catalase, glutathione peroxidase, and glucose‐6‐phosphate dehydrogenase. The sources of ROS are marked in red, while the antioxidant components are marked in blue." [ref=e172]:
                  - heading "FIGURE 2." [level=3] [ref=e173]
                  - paragraph [ref=e174]:
                    - img "FIGURE 2" [ref=e175]
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e177] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/mco270600-fig-0002/
                  - paragraph [ref=e179]: The production and elimination of reactive oxygen species (ROS). There are multiple intracellular sources of ROS, including the electron transport chain, monoamine oxidase, P66shc, the process of protein folding, cytochrome P450 enzymes, peroxisomal oxidases, and nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate oxidases (NOXs). Correspondingly, there are various non‐enzymatic antioxidants and antioxidant enzymes within the cells, including superoxide dismutase (SOD), catalase, glutathione peroxidase, and glucose‐6‐phosphate dehydrogenase. The sources of ROS are marked in red, while the antioxidant components are marked in blue.
                - generic [ref=e180]:
                  - heading "2.1. The Intracellular Sources of ROS" [level=3] [ref=e181]
                  - paragraph [ref=e182]:
                    - text: The ROS production and relative contributions from different sources vary with cell types and physiological conditions. The presence of both ROS consumers and ROS producers in the same subcellular structure means that only a net rate of ROS can be detected, which is much lower than the absolute rate of ROS production. However, detection of the net rate can indicate whether a particular subcellular structure exports or imports ROS [
                    - link "35" [ref=e183] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0035"
                    - text: "]. Mitochondria are normally regarded as the main source of ROS in mammalian cells, whereas other subcellular structures also produce abundant ROS. Therefore, exploring other sites of intracellular ROS production is conducive to the precise regulation of oxidative stress."
                  - paragraph [ref=e184]:
                    - text: Mitochondria serve as the main oxygen consumers and the primary source of ROS within cells. The electron transport chain (ETC), a series of electron‐carrier proteins located in the mitochondrial inner membrane, is the main contributor to mitochondrial ROS [
                    - link "36" [ref=e185] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0036"
                    - text: "]. ETC receives hydrogen ions from nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide (NADH) or flavin adenine dinucleotide (FADH"
                    - subscript [ref=e186]: "2"
                    - text: ) and deliver electrons to oxygen, ultimately generating ROS [
                    - link "37" [ref=e187] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0037"
                    - text: "]. This process mainly occurs in complexes I and III. In complex I, NADH is oxidized to produce electrons, which are then transferred to oxygen to generate O"
                    - subscript [ref=e188]: "2"
                    - text: •
                    - superscript [ref=e189]: −
                    - text: "["
                    - link "38" [ref=e190] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0038"
                    - text: "]. In complex III, oxygen interacts with semiquinone and produces large amounts of O"
                    - subscript [ref=e191]: "2"
                    - text: •
                    - superscript [ref=e192]: −
                    - text: "["
                    - link "39" [ref=e193] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0039"
                    - text: "]. Subsequently, O"
                    - subscript [ref=e194]: "2"
                    - text: •
                    - superscript [ref=e195]: −
                    - text: will be rapidly converted into H
                    - subscript [ref=e196]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e197]: "2"
                    - text: by manganese superoxide dismutase (MnSOD) within the mitochondrial matrix and then transmitted to the cytoplasm to participate in other biological reactions [
                    - link "40" [ref=e198] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0040"
                    - text: "]. In addition to ETC, other ROS production sites exist in mitochondria including monoamine oxidase (MAO) and p66shc. MAO is localized to the outer mitochondrial membrane, where it catalyzes the oxidative deamination of aromatic amines such as catecholamines and generates H"
                    - subscript [ref=e199]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e200]: "2"
                    - text: "["
                    - link "41" [ref=e201] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0041"
                    - text: "]. Physiologically, MAO plays a crucial role in the termination of neurotransmitter signal conduction in the nervous system and serves as a biomarker for neurodegenerative and psychiatric disorders ["
                    - link "42" [ref=e202] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0042"
                    - text: "]. Exacerbated MAO activity in pathological conditions can induce oxidative stress and cause harmful effects such as cardiovascular diseases ["
                    - link "43" [ref=e203] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0043"
                    - text: "]. p66Shc, a member of the Src homologous‐collagen homologue adaptor protein family, is an oxidoreductase that produces ROS in a mitochondria‐dependent manner. Specifically, p66Shc can generate ROS through the oxidation of cytochrome c by utilizing the reducing equivalents of the mitochondrial ETC, playing a crucial role in carcinogenesis and aging ["
                    - link "44" [ref=e204] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0044"
                    - text: "]."
                  - paragraph [ref=e205]:
                    - text: Endoplasmic reticulum is the largest intracellular organelle comprising a variety of tubules and sheet‐like structures spanning the cytoplasm. Physiologically, endoplasmic reticulum is responsible for several cellular functions including protein biosynthesis, folding, and transport, calcium storage and gated release, and lipid biosynthesis [
                    - link "45" [ref=e206] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0045"
                    - text: "]. The formation of intramolecular disulfide bond is one of the most common post‐translational modifications occurring within the endoplasmic reticulum and is crucial for the production of correctly folded proteins. A highly oxidized environment is essential for this process, which is maintained by a series of enzymatic components especially endoplasmic reticulum oxidoreductase 1 (ERO1) and protein disulfide isomerase (PDI) ["
                    - link "46" [ref=e207] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0046"
                    - text: "]. ERO1 can utilize flavin adenine dinucleotide (FAD) as an electron acceptor to promote the formation of disulfide bond, which is then transferred to nascent protein substrates via PDI. Due to the reoxidation of the reduced FAD through reaction with oxygen, the ERO1 activity contributes to the generation of H"
                    - subscript [ref=e208]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e209]: "2"
                    - text: as a by‐product [
                    - link "47" [ref=e210] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0047"
                    - text: "]. In addition to the protein folding, enzymatic reactions within endoplasmic reticulum can also produce ROS. The cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzymes are a diverse group of heme monooxygenases that play a significant role in drug metabolism and lipid biosynthesis. In the presence of oxygen, CYP enzymes catalyze the hydroxylation of a hydrocarbon substrate with the assistance of electron donors. Nevertheless, ROS may leak in the intermediate steps of the reaction, known as “reaction decoupling,” which leads to elevated ROS levels and incomplete substrate oxidation ["
                    - link "48" [ref=e211] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0048"
                    - text: "]. There are various factors that determine the coupling efficiency of CYP reaction, such as the substrates and reaction microenvironment. For example, the stimulation of nasal epithelial cells with PM2.5 can increase ROS production through CYP1A1 ["
                    - link "49" [ref=e212] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0049"
                    - text: "]."
                  - paragraph [ref=e213]:
                    - text: Peroxisomes are single membrane‐bound organelles that contain a series of different enzymes. Owing to their high content of peroxisomal oxidases and antioxidant enzymes, peroxisomes play a central role in the dynamic balance of ROS generation and scavenging by catalyzing the dynamic spin of ROS, especially H
                    - subscript [ref=e214]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e215]: "2"
                    - text: "["
                    - link "50" [ref=e216] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0050"
                    - text: "]. Peroxisomes are multifunctional organelles that participate in the α‐oxidation of branched‐chain fatty acids, the β‐oxidation of very long chain fatty acids, the oxidation of D‐amino acids and polyamines, and the biosynthesis of phospholipids and bile acids ["
                    - link "51" [ref=e217] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0051"
                    - text: "]. As by‐products of these metabolic processes, a variety of oxidases present in peroxisomes produce ROS such as O"
                    - subscript [ref=e218]: "2"
                    - text: •
                    - superscript [ref=e219]: −
                    - text: and H
                    - subscript [ref=e220]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e221]: "2."
                    - text: Acyl‐CoA oxidases are the most abundant oxidases among almost all peroxisomes independent of the cell type. In addition, ROS‐producing oxidases include D‐amino acid oxidase, D‐aspartate oxidase, urate oxidase, and xanthine oxidase [
                    - link "52" [ref=e222] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0052"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "53" [ref=e223] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0053"
                    - text: "]. Peroxisomes play an integral role in metabolic processes, thereby interacting and coordinating with other subcellular organelles, such as mitochondria, endoplasmic reticulum, lysosomes, and nucleus, which may also involve redox signaling ["
                    - link "54" [ref=e224] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0054"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "55" [ref=e225] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0055"
                    - text: "]. Notably, a recent study has shown that peroxisomes play a direct role in maintaining mitochondrial redox homeostasis through contact‐mediated ROS transfer. During this process, ACBD5 and PTPIP51 form the contact between peroxisomes and mitochondria. When the oxidative stress occurs in mitochondria, the percentage of contacts increase to transfer ROS from mitochondria to the lumen of peroxisomes ["
                    - link "56" [ref=e226] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0056"
                    - text: "]. This finding reveals the direct mechanism by which peroxisomes promote mitochondrial health and deepens our understanding of ROS transfer between subcellular organelles."
                  - paragraph [ref=e227]:
                    - text: Nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NADPH) oxidases (NOXs) are a family of transmembrane enzymes proteins mainly located in the plasma membrane [
                    - link "57" [ref=e228] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0057"
                    - text: "]. NOXs can generate electrons from NADPH and react with oxygen to produce O"
                    - subscript [ref=e229]: "2"
                    - text: •
                    - superscript [ref=e230]: −
                    - text: and H
                    - subscript [ref=e231]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e232]: "2"
                    - text: ", serving as the major source of nonmitochondrial ROS. The NOX family is comprised of seven members: NOX1 to NOX5, DUOX1, and DUOX2, which function either independently or in combination with one or more auxiliary subunits ["
                    - link "58" [ref=e233] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0058"
                    - text: "]. NOX1 to NOX3 require assembly of cytosolic regulatory subunits for activatoin, such as NOXA1, NOXO1, Tks 4/5, p40"
                    - superscript [ref=e234]: phox
                    - text: ", p47"
                    - superscript [ref=e235]: phox
                    - text: ", p67"
                    - superscript [ref=e236]: phox
                    - text: ", and Rac. NOX4 has constitutive activity, and its catalytic activity can be enhanced by Poldip2 and Tks 4/5. In addition, the catalytic subunits of NOX1 to NOX4 combine with p22"
                    - superscript [ref=e237]: phox
                    - text: to stabilize the complex. On the contrary, the activation of NOX5, DUOX1, and DUOX2 is calcium dependent and not dependent on the binding with p22
                    - superscript [ref=e238]: phox
                    - text: "["
                    - link "59" [ref=e239] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0059"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "60" [ref=e240] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0060"
                    - text: "]. The ROS generated by NOXs plays an integral role in various physiological processes, including innate immunity, redox signaling, and hormone production ["
                    - link "61" [ref=e241] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0061"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "62" [ref=e242] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0062"
                    - text: "]."
                - generic [ref=e243]:
                  - heading "2.2. The Multilayered Antioxidant Defense Network" [level=3] [ref=e244]
                  - paragraph [ref=e245]:
                    - text: Corresponding to the production of ROS, organisms have developed an antioxidant system to prevent the excessive accumulation of ROS and defend against oxidative stress. The antioxidant enzymes play a major role in combating the massive buildup of ROS due to their antioxidant activity. Meanwhile, the non‐enzymatic antioxidants are also important components of the antioxidant system. In reaction to oxidants and other electrophilic reagents, the antioxidant system is reinforced, thereby strengthening the capacity to resist oxidative stress [
                    - link "63" [ref=e246] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0063"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "64" [ref=e247] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0064"
                    - text: "]. Hence, enhancing the antioxidant system has been the principal strategy of antioxidant treatment."
                  - paragraph [ref=e248]:
                    - text: Superoxide dismutase (SOD) is the first line of antioxidant system and catalyzes the dismutation of O
                    - subscript [ref=e249]: "2"
                    - text: •
                    - superscript [ref=e250]: −
                    - text: into H
                    - subscript [ref=e251]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e252]: "2"
                    - text: ", which is then decomposed by CAT and GPX. There are three distinct members of SOD family in mammals, including Cu/Zn‐SOD (SOD1), Mn‐SOD (SOD2), and extracellular SOD (EC‐SOD/SOD3), which exhibit structural diversity and all have free radical scavenging activities ["
                    - link "65" [ref=e253] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0065"
                    - text: "]. They have a wide range of subcellular localization: SOD1 is located in the cytoplasm, while SOD2 (Mn‐SOD) is found in the mitochondria, and SOD3 (EC‐SOD) is distributed in the extracellular matrix ["
                    - link "66" [ref=e254] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0066"
                    - text: "]. By virtue of this, the SOD family serves as an efficient and hierarchical antioxidant system. CAT, which is typically found in cellular peroxisomes, utilizes iron as a key component of the active site for the decomposition of H"
                    - subscript [ref=e255]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e256]: "2"
                    - text: into oxygen and water. The three‐dimensional structure of CAT includes heme as a prosthetic group, which is essential for its catalytic efficiency [
                    - link "67" [ref=e257] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0067"
                    - text: "]. GPX, which is primarily located in the cytoplasm and mitochondria, is an enzyme containing four units of selenium as cofactors. With the help of glutathione (GSH), GPX can reduce H"
                    - subscript [ref=e258]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e259]: "2"
                    - text: or lipid peroxides [
                    - link "68" [ref=e260] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0068"
                    - text: "]. In addition, glucose‐6‐phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PDH) is a housekeeping enzyme that takes part in the pentose phosphate pathway and catalyzes the conversion of NADP"
                    - superscript [ref=e261]: +
                    - text: into NADPH, which is crucial for reducing oxidative stress within cells [
                    - link "69" [ref=e262] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0069"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "70" [ref=e263] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0070"
                    - text: "]."
                  - paragraph [ref=e264]:
                    - text: Generally, the catalytic efficiency of antioxidant enzymes is much higher than that of non‐enzymatic antioxidants. However, the subcellular localization or lack of metal ions may limit the antioxidant effect of enzymes. In this case, the non‐enzymatic antioxidants take over the fight against ROS. The non‐enzymatic antioxidants mainly consist of small molecular antioxidants with a molecular weight of less than 1 kDa, such as GSH, coenzyme Q10, vitamin C, vitamin E, carotenoids, and phenolic compounds. They can be directly involved in scavenging ROS to protect organisms from oxidative damage and participate in several physiological processes (Table
                    - link "1" [ref=e265] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#mco270600-tbl-0001"
                    - text: ).
                  - generic [ref=e266]:
                    - heading "TABLE 1." [level=4] [ref=e267]
                    - paragraph [ref=e269]: The main non‐enzymatic antioxidants in vivo.
                    - table [ref=e271]:
                      - rowgroup [ref=e278]:
                        - row "Antioxidant General description Mechanism of action Synthesized in vivo Reference" [ref=e279]:
                          - columnheader "Antioxidant" [ref=e280]
                          - columnheader "General description" [ref=e281]
                          - columnheader "Mechanism of action" [ref=e282]
                          - columnheader "Synthesized in vivo" [ref=e283]
                          - columnheader "Reference" [ref=e284]
                      - rowgroup [ref=e285]:
                        - row "Glutathione A tripeptide composed of glutamic acid, cysteine, and glycine Glutathione provides reducing equivalents for glutathione peroxidases and directly eliminates reactive oxygen species (ROS) Yes [71]" [ref=e286]:
                          - cell "Glutathione" [ref=e287]
                          - cell "A tripeptide composed of glutamic acid, cysteine, and glycine" [ref=e288]
                          - cell "Glutathione provides reducing equivalents for glutathione peroxidases and directly eliminates reactive oxygen species (ROS)" [ref=e289]
                          - cell "Yes" [ref=e290]
                          - cell "[71]" [ref=e291]:
                            - text: "["
                            - link "71" [ref=e292] [cursor=pointer]:
                              - /url: "#mco270600-bib-0071"
                            - text: "]"
                        - row "Coenzyme Q10 An isoprenylated benzoquinone also known as ubiquinone Coenzyme Q10 directly scavenges ROS and inhibits lipid peroxidation Yes [72]" [ref=e293]:
                          - cell "Coenzyme Q10" [ref=e294]
                          - cell "An isoprenylated benzoquinone also known as ubiquinone" [ref=e295]
                          - cell "Coenzyme Q10 directly scavenges ROS and inhibits lipid peroxidation" [ref=e296]
